先週（11/2〜11/9）発表の主な経済指標
⽶国雇⽤は安定も、AI曝露度の⾼い職業で若年雇⽤が弱含
み。雇⽤不安が⺠主党躍進を後押しした可能性。AI関連株が
調整するなか、韓国中銀は投資減速のアジアへの波及を警戒
所定内給与は物価上昇率を上回る伸びが持続。冬季ボーナス
も押し上げ。電気・ガス料⾦補助再開は消費の追い⾵に。⼀⽅、
消費回復が進めば基調的な物価上昇圧⼒を強める可能性
注目点② 賃上げの波及と物価対策が日本の消費を下支え注目点① 米AI投資拡大の裏で広がる「不安」と「リスク」
⾮農業部⾨雇⽤者数は市場予想を上回り、失業
率も低下。ISM景況指数は製造業・⾮製造業とも
に拡⼤圏を維持。雇⽤・企業活動の底堅さの⼀⽅
で、消費者マインドは持ち直すも依然として低⽔準

（出所）米労働省より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
失業率
米国：雇用統計は良好な結果。一方で、賃金上昇もインフレ見合いで見劣り
 10月の非農業部門雇用者数は前月差＋16.5万人と市場予想（同＋6.5万人）を大きく上回る結果
―過 去2カ月も合わせて8.4万人の上方修正。業種別では専門ビジネスサービスやヘルスケアが全体をけん引
― 時間当たり賃金も前月比＋0.4％（前年比＋3.1％）と着実に増加。ただしインフレ率に割り負けている状況が継続
 失業率は4.1％と前月（4.2％）から小幅低下も、失業期間は長期化
― 失業期間の中央値は11.9週（9月：同11.5週）と長期化。27週以上の長期失業者も増加基調維持。企業の採用率は低下

傾向が続いており、労働者が失業状態から脱しにくい傾向が継続
3
（出所）米労働省より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
非農業部門雇用者数
失業率は低位安定
雇用者数は市場予想を大きく上回る
過去2カ月もそれぞれ上方修正

（年）
米国：AI普及の影響は若年層に偏在。雇用機会への不安が拡大
 AI曝露度別の雇用指数をみると、22～34歳では曝露度の高い職業の雇用が相対的に弱含み
― AIの影響がキャリア形成段階の若年層に偏っている可能性
 若年層では、AIによる雇用代替に加え、物価高など生活面の不安も根強い状況
― AIを否定的に捉える割合も若い世代ほど高く、AI普及への警戒感がうかがえる状況

4
（注） 上図の若年層とは18～29歳。経済状況は回答者の主観的な評価
（出所） Harvard Youth Poll、ABC News、Washington Post、Ipsosより、みずほ総合研
究所調査部作成
AI曝露度別雇用指数
年齢別AIの認識：「AIは社会に悪い」と回答した割合
（注） 1．AI曝露度とは各職業の業務が生成AIの影響を受ける度合いを示す。職業を曝露度
に応じて5分位に分類し、低・中・高はそれぞれ第1・第3・第5分位を指す
2．年齢層別の雇用指数は、米国雇用統計の家計調査における年齢別雇用者数をウェ
イトに加重平均して算出。年齢区分が一致しない部分は、各階級内の雇用者数が均
等に分布すると仮定して按分
（出所）スタンフォード大学デジタル経済研究所、ADP Researchより、みずほ総合研究所調査
部作成
中堅・シニア層（35歳以上）
若年層（22～34歳）
若年層が感じる経済的脅威
若年層ほどAIへの反感が強い
若年層に偏る雇用への影響

（年/月）
米国：若年層の経済的不安が中間選挙の投票行動にも影響か
 2026年中間選挙では「物価高」や「雇用」への不安が若年層の主要な関心事項に
― こうした経済的不安が共和党政権への不満につながった可能性
― 年齢別の投票動向では、若年層を中心に民主党支持が優勢。生活費負担や将来の雇用機会への不安が投票行動に
影響した模様
― なお、議会でも世代交代が進展。若い世代の政策選好が今後の政策形成に与える影響にも注目

### Page 7

（参考）民主党が掲げる主な政策
 勝利した民主党は、若年層の不満とも重なる生活費負担の軽減（アフォーダビリティ）を共通の政策課題に位置付け
 具体的な政策手段を巡っては、中道派と左派・進歩派の間で違い
― 中道派は競争促進や規制緩和、財政規律を重視する一方、左派・進歩派は大企業への規制・課税強化や政府支出の
拡大を主張。今後の党内調整が焦点に
6

（年）

アジア：AI投資減速の影響が域内の貿易ネットワークを通じて波及するリスク
 韓国中銀はAI関連投資の拡大に伴う過剰投資やレバレッジの高まり、循環金融などの金融リスクを警戒
― AI産業の期待収益の過大評価や最終的なリスク所在把握が困難になり、リスクが過小評価される可能性があると指摘
― また、米国企業のAI投資が減速する悲観シナリオでは、韓国の半導体輸出が大幅に減少し、GDP成長率を0.4％Pt押し
下げると試算
― 韓国のAI関連財貿易は中国・台湾との取引が4割超。アジア域内で形成された貿易ネットワークを通じて、AI投資減速の
影響が広範に波及する可能性

日本：物価対策の再開が家計負担を軽減。消費の回復を後押し
9
 政府は冬場の電気・ガス料金補助の再開を決定。標準的な家庭で計7,000円程度の負担軽減を見込む
― 一方、ガソリン価格の補助は段階的に縮小（基準額を毎月5円ずつ引き上げ）。電気・ガス料金補助金の効果を一部減殺
 補助金を巡る政策変更は、コアCPIの前年比を最大0.4％程度押し下げ
― 賃金上昇に加え、政策による物価負担軽減が家計の実質購買力を下支え
― 物価対策がCPIを押し下げる一方、消費回復を通じて基調的な物価上昇圧力が強まる可能性には留意が必要

（注） ガソリンと同額の補助を実施している軽油・重油・灯油、ガソリン補助額の4割相当

欧州：10月消費者物価はエネルギー主導で加速。企業は販売価格の引き上げを計画
10
 10月消費者物価（HICP）は前年比＋4.0％と、エネルギー主導で前月（同＋3.8％）から加速
― 一方、コアは同＋2.5％と前月から不変。エネルギー高の二次的な影響はいまだ顕在化せず
 コスト上昇圧力が高まるなか、企業は販売価格の引き上げを計画。高止まりするインフレにECBは警戒姿勢を維持

中国：政策金利をリバースレポ翌日物に変更。銀行貸出金利の新たな決定メカニズムを模索
 中国人民銀行は政策金利をリバースレポ翌日物に変更（11/2）。短期市場金利の誘導精度を高める狙い
― リバースレポ7日物から切り替え、翌日物のオペを毎営業日実施。最優遇貸出金利（LPR）も翌日物金利を参照へ
― インターバンク市場における債券担保付レポ翌日物金利（DR001）が誘導目標と明言。短期金利の安定化を図る考え
 銀行貸出金利の新たな決定メカニズムを模索。DR001を参照して貸出金利を決定する方法を試験的に導入

― 背景には、銀行貸出の指標金利であるLPRが「最優遇」から乖離し、LPRより低利での貸出比率が上昇している実態
― 今後、財務力のある企業は銀行と協議の上でDR001かLPRを選択し、小規模企業や個人には引き続きLPRを適用へ

中国：景況感は再び悪化し、2カ月ぶりの節目割れ。製造業は需給ともに振るわず
 10月の景況感は再び悪化。総合PMIは49.7と前月（50.7）から大幅に低下し、2カ月ぶりの節目割れ
― うち非製造業PMIが49.7と前月（50.2）から低下も、宿泊やスポーツ・娯楽が持ち直し、情報サービスは堅調だった
― 建設業PMIは48.0と前月（50.3）から急低下。中国物流購買聯合会は建設工事の着工が遅れ気味と指摘

― 中東情勢に伴う価格上昇に一服感。原材料価格は56.8（同：60.8）、出荷価格は51.7（同：54.0）まで戻す

中国：PPIは生産財が急上昇も、川下への価格転嫁進まず。 CPIは食品価格が下押し要因に
 10月のPPIは前年比＋5.7％（前月：同＋5.0％）。中東情勢の影響による資源・エネルギー関連業種の価格上昇続く
― 石油・石炭加工（同＋29.9％）と非鉄金属加工（同＋18.6％）、化学品（同＋16.4％）が引き続き高い伸び

 CPIは同＋0.7％と、前月（同＋0.9％）から減速。食品価格の下落（▲2.9％）が下押し要因に（▲0.49％Pt）

― 他方、燃料価格高騰により、ガソリンは同＋22.4％（前月：同＋16.6％）まで上昇し、全体を＋0.63％Pt押し上げ
― 食品・エネルギーを除くコアは同＋0.8％（前月：＋1.0％）と弱いが、通信機器はメモリー高騰で同＋16.1％の伸び

### Page 15

アジア：ASEAN5の製造業景況感は概ね堅調。原油高など仕入コスト上昇の影響は残存
 ASEAN5の10月の製造業景況感は、フィリピンを除き改善圏。受注・生産は好調も、コスト高が継続
― タイ、ベトナムは高水準で推移し、インドネシアは回復が鮮明。一方、フィリピンが基準となる50を下回る水準
― S&Pはリリース資料において、新規受注の増加が生産を支えたものの、仕入コスト上昇と原料調達・物流の制約を指摘
 ASEAN5では、生産者物価（PPI）が高止まり。原油高の影響が残存
― 原油高が続く中、石炭・石油に加えて化学やプラ・ゴム等の製品価格にも上昇圧力が継続
― 仕入コストの高止まりが、企業収益や生産活動の重荷となるリスクに留意が必要

 ASEAN5の一部の国で財政収支が悪化

― フィリピンは税収の伸び悩み、タイは景気下支えに向けた公共投資拡大や予算執行の加速などにより歳出が大きく増加

― タイは公的債務残高が法定上限（GDP比70％）に接近。マレーシアも政府債務残高が高水準
― フィリピンは財政赤字の縮小が課題。インドネシアは財政赤字上限（GDP比3％）が制約要因

地圖設計的約8席
• 失靈原因：新選區把D選民打散至拉美裔占多數的「安全R區」，但拉美裔回流
使這些選區由安全轉為競爭，形成反效果
• 象徵：共和黨眾院競選委員會主席Hudson於北卡第9選區落選

電源 6／10  主張暫停(含附條件) 7／10  租稅優惠退場(含附 條件) 民主黨2028盤算：管而不禁，以稅優縮減、開發資格審查(實績、財務擔 保、自備電源）與電價成本分攤控管；市場普遍皆認為全國性禁令不太可 能，但會拖慢建設時程

【德州AIDC政策】
德州Hinojosa主張「先立規、再放行」，並非全面反對AIDC
資料來源：Hinojosa競選網站、Fox 4、Texas Monthly、德州州長辦公室、LegiScan、NBC News、台新投顧整理，資料時間：2026/11
8
• 立場比較：稽核後放行vs.立法前暫停
項目 Abbott（R，卸任） Hinojosa（D，當選）
過去立場 曾稱德州為「AI發展的中心」，積極招商 ；6月起轉向管制 2017年州眾議員任內，放寬資料中心銷售稅豁免 就業認定的HB 4038於眾院以144:0通過
暫停方式 8月令PUC、ERCOT稽核併網佇列；9/21 令TCEQ停發資料中心許可至稽核完成 就任後暫停新資料中心許可，至州議會完成立法為 止(有期限、非永久)
成本與補貼 主張廢除銷售稅豁免與其他優惠；大型業 者每年申報用電用水 停止補貼、「讓資料中心自己付錢」；業者須證明 住戶電費水費不因電網升級轉嫁
社區與勞工 主張禁止在鄉村社區興建 居民決定「是否及如何」興建；禁NDA；工會勞 工；威脅供水的用水合約無效

• Hinojosa主張非「反AIDC」
• 暫停有終點：「我可以下令暫停，但只會維持到
法律通過為止」（10/13）
• 六大優先全是條件：工會就業、社區參與、透明
、電水費不轉嫁、停止補貼、用水優先，未主張
禁止AI或拆除既有案
• 攻擊重點是「無規則」：「企業在毫無規則下運
作」，矛頭指向Abbott招商與金主，而非產業本
身
• 曾支持優惠：任內HB 4038無異議通過，放寬資
料中心稅收優惠
• 州長權力邊界
能做 做不到

Abbott模式）

FY29預估33億美元）

ACA／Medicaid
管理式醫療 MS熱力圖於D橫掃情境評為正向；2028單一支付制為尾部風險 CNC、OSCR、MOH
綠能／儲能
公用事業 D推動綠能稅優作為談判籌碼；資料中心電價壓力轉向「誰付費」 FSLR、NEE、ENPH、BEP

配置重點：醫療服務為藍潮最直接受惠，近期市場提前反應主要由估值驅動，明年或有望看見獲利改善推動股價上漲，可逢回布局當衛星部位；綠能受惠

於政策但利率敏感，偏好具儲能與大型負載合約的公用事業（低電價通膨州、工商業用電占比高）；消費端以平價、必需消費優於關稅敏感的非必需消費

子產業 觀點 核心理由 代表標的
大型 CSP 偏多 基本面無虞、評等與現金流可吸收成本自負條款，州級政策逆風下，市占反而集中 MSFT、GOOGL、AMZN、 META
AI 晶片龍頭、設備、技術先進
具護城河者 偏多 AI融資疑慮壓抑估值，惟技術壁壘與獲利韌性提供支撐 NVDA、ASML、TSM
漲價半導體 中性 漲價推升獲利至循環高峰，惟短期CSP現金流有限，Capex上修幅度放緩，估值承壓， 需仰賴總經環境風險緩解，可採區間操作 光通訊、MLCC、記憶體
高槓桿AIDC開發商 偏空 翻藍州 GW 級DC曝險高、BBB-評級低於擔保門檻、再融資壓力及被加大監管的企業 ORCL、SpaceX
NeoCloud 偏空 站點分散、高槓桿，CDS 已達困境水準，信用傳染風險最高 CRWV、NBIS

法案 黨派 影響產業 進度
國會貿易權改革法案/撤銷特定關稅法案 民主黨 輸美消費品、電子產品 兩案均已提案

先進 AI 晶片出口監督法案
(AI OVERWATCH) 跨黨派 AI 晶片、運算能力供應 鏈 委員會已通過
生物安全法案(BIOSECURE) 跨黨派 生技研發與製造服務 已成法
對外投資國安法案(COINS) 跨黨派 半導體、AI 等敏感科技 已成法
• 國會對中貿易、科技與投資重點法案 • 中國科技與生技龍頭海外營收占比
• 晶片出口許可可能面臨更嚴格的審查：新國會就任後，民主黨可透過聽證與調查，要求商務部說明對中晶片出口許可的核發依據。
若商務部加強終端用途核驗，企業可能先面臨審批拉長、交付延後。

• 科技管制有跨黨支持，新增限制仍有立法門檻：先進晶片與半導體設備管制已有跨黨派支持，新增限制仍須兩院通過相同文本，並
面對總統可能否決。BIOSECURE 與 COINS 已成法，後續分別看聯邦採購限制涵蓋哪些供應商，以及對外投資規則涵蓋哪些交易。
• 強迫勞動查核可能成為國會監督重點：民主黨可能加強監督《維吾爾強迫勞動預防法》（UFLPA）的執行情況。若美國海關加嚴查
核，紡織與太陽能產品的輸美供應鏈可能面臨更多原料溯源舉證要求，通關時間也可能拉長。

レポート

2026 年冬のボーナス見通し

～企業の好業績と人手不足を背景に堅調な増加が続く～

調査部 主席研究員 小林 真一郎 
研究員 小林 啓介

○ 2026 年冬の民間企業（調査産業計・事業所規模 5 人以上）のボーナスは、前年比＋5.0%と 6 年連続で増
加が見込まれる。好調な企業業績と堅調な雇用情勢が追い風となり、大幅な伸びになるだろう。 
○ 支給労働者割合は 87.3%（前年差＋0.8%ポイント）と 2 年ぶりに上昇するだろう。同割合はコロナ前の水準
を超えて回復しており、雇用者数の増加が続く中、ボーナスが支給される事業所で働く労働者の数は 4,577
万人（前年比＋1.9%）と、4 年連続で過去最多を更新する見込みである。 
○ 一人当たり支給額と支給労働者数の増加を受け、ボーナスの支給総額は20.4 兆円（前年比＋7.0%）と 6 年
連続で増加しよう。 ボーナスの支給総額の伸びは物価上昇率を大きく上回っており 、個人消費の回復を下
支えすると期待される。 
○ 2026 年冬の国家公務員（管理職および非常勤を除く一般行政職）のボーナス（期末・勤勉手当）の平均支
給額は 77 万 9,100 円（前年比＋4.4%）と前年冬に続き、大きめの増加が見込まれる。景気回復に伴う民間
給与の増加が公務員にも反映される状況が続き 、基本給、ボーナス支給月数とも引き上げられるとみられる。  
2026 年冬のボーナス見通し

(注 1)民間企業（調査産業計・事業所規模5 人以上）は、賞与を支給する事業所で働く全常用労働者（当該事業所で賞与
の支給を受けていない労働者も含む）の平均 
(注 2)国家公務員は、管理職および非常勤を除く一般行政職の平均 
(注 3)支給労働者数は、賞与を支給する事業所で働く全常用労働者（当該事業所で賞与の支給を受けていない労働者も
含む）の数 
(注 4)支給総額は一人当たり支給額に支給労働者数を掛け合わせた値 
(出所)厚生労働省「毎月勤労統計」、内閣人事局資料などを基に当社予測

（年度、夏季賞与）
1. 2026 年夏のボーナス ～企業の好業績を背景に 5 年連続の増加

厚生労働省「毎月勤労統計調査」によると、民間企業（調査産業計・事業所規模 5 人以上）における 2026
年夏のボーナスの一人当たり支給額は、前年比 ＋5.3%と 5 年連続で増加した （図表 1）。前年（202 5 年）の
伸びである同＋2.9%を大きく 上回っており 、2026 年は企業の 好業績 を背景にボーナス は堅調に増加し た。 
業種別では、 製造業（前年比＋ 5.8%）、非製造業（同＋5 .3%）ともに増加した。内訳をみると、 生活関連 サ
ービス 等（前年比＋ 25.7%）、 建設業（ 同＋14.4%）、 学術研究等 （同＋ 12.9%）などが 大幅に 増加し、 全体
の数字を押し上げた 。一方で、 複合サービス事業（同－2 .1%）は減少した。 
ボーナス支給月数（所定内給与に対する比率）は 1.10 カ月分と前年から 0.02 カ月増加した 。また、ボー
ナスが支給された労働者の割合 1は 85.1%（前年差 ＋0.5%ポイント ）と小幅に上昇した。 雇用者数が 順調に
増加して 過去最高水準 を更新し ており 、ボーナスが支給された 労働者数は前年比 ＋1.4%と増加し た。 夏の
ボーナス の支給総額（平均給与額×支給労働者数）は 19.9 兆円（前年比＋ 6.8%）と過去最高を記録した。

図表 1 夏のボーナス実績：平均支給額（前年比）と支給月数

（注 2）支給月数は所定内給与に対する支給割合 
（出所）厚生労働省「毎月勤労統計」

1 労働者の総数に対して、ボーナスが支給される事業所で働く労働者（当該事業所でボーナスの支給を受けていない労働者も含む）が占
める割合。

2. 2026 年冬のボーナス見通し

(1) 民間企業 ～ 企業の好業績と労働需給の逼迫を追い風に、6 年連続で増加する見込み

厚生労働省「毎月勤労統計調査」ベースで見た民間企業（調査産業計・事業所規模 5 人以上）の 2026 年冬
のボーナスの一人当たり平均支給額は 44 万 6,252 円（前年比＋5.0%）と 6 年連続での増加を予想する（図表
2）。企業規模を問わず、製造業、非製造業とも一人当たり平均支給額は増加すると予測する。 
今年の冬のボーナスが順調な増加を続けるとみられる背景に、企業業績の好調さと雇用情勢の堅調さがある。
企業の経常利益（全規模、金融保険業を除く全産業、季節調整値）は、2026 年 4～6 月期に過去最高を更新す
るなど好調に推移している。今後イラン情勢の展開次第では業績に下押し圧力がかかるリスクはあるが、 冬のボ
ーナスへの影響は軽微だろう。 
また、人手不足は深刻化し、労働需給は逼迫している。日銀短観における雇用人員判断 DI は対面型サービ
ス業を中心に大幅な「不足」超が続き、企業の人手不足感が強まる中、完全失業率は2％台半ばと低水準での推
移が続いている。

図表 2 冬のボーナス予測：平均支給額（前年比）と支給月数

（注 1）調査産業計、事業所規模 5 人以上  
（注 2）支給月数は所定内給与に対する支給割合 
（出所）厚生労働省「毎月勤労統計」

の水準を大きく超えた支給労働者割合に加え、雇用者数が堅調に増加していることから、ボーナスが支給される
労働者数は 4,577 万人（前年比＋1.9%）と、非製造業を中心に増加し、4 年連続で過去最多を更新する公算が
大きい（図表 3）。

支給総額の伸びは物価上昇率を大きく上回っており、 ボーナス支給総額の増加が 個人消費の回復を下支えす
る要因になると期待される。

予測
(2) 公務員 ～ 基本給、支給月数ともプラスに寄与し、ボーナスは 5 年連続で増加

内閣人事局の発表によると、国家公務員（管理職および非常勤を除く一般行政職）の 2026 年夏のボーナス
（期末・勤勉手当）は 73 万 8,500 円（前年比＋4.5%）と 4 年連続で増加した。昨年の人事院勧告に基づく給与
法改正でボーナスの基準となる基本給が前年比＋3.3%と増加したほか、ボーナス支給月数が昨夏から 0.025 カ
月引き上げられた。 
2026 年冬のボーナスの平均支給額は 77 万 9,100 円（前年比＋4.4%）と、5 年連続で増加すると予測する（図
表 8）。今年の人事院勧告に沿った給与法改正が行われることを前提に、平均給与額が増加（＋3.3%）するととも
に、ボーナス支給月数が昨冬から 0.025 カ月分（勤勉手当、期末手当がそれぞれ 0.0125 カ月分）引き上げられ
ると想定する。支給額は夏冬通じて過去最高を更新し 、景気回復に伴う民間給与の増加が公務員にも反映され
る状況が続くとみられる。今後も、民間の賃金上昇傾向が続く中、公務員のボーナスも高めの伸びが続く見込み
である。

M
Update
Vanguard International Semiconductor  | Asia
Pacific
Strong demand but margin
headwinds ahead; UW
2027 margin could be under pressure from depreciation: With the ramp up of the
new Singapore fab (VSMC), the company expected  depreciation to increase in 2027,
however, the company did not share the margin dilution guidance. As we noted in a
previous report, we still expect the margin dilution from the Singapore capacity
could be high-single digit for 2027, higher than investors' expectation of low-mid

single digit.
2027 demand remains strong: Despite the slowdown of non-AI demand, the
company expects both 8-inch and 12-inch loading rate to be >90% in 2027 and the
company is under capacity conversion and hopes to keep pushing up the utilization
by capacity optimization. 2027 AI related revenue could also see a significant
increase from interposer production, which could be 20-30% of 2027 revenue, in
our view.
4Q26 revenue could be up mid-single digit QoQ: The company guided 4Q26 wafer

shipments  to be flat to up 2% QoQ given the bottleneck from certain equipment,
but wafer ASP could increase by 3-5% QoQ. Guidance is slightly higher than our 3%
QoQ revenue growth forecast.

Key Takeaways
3Q26 results were in-line with both MSe and consensus.
VSMC break-even point could be slightly earlier than 30kwpm thanks to the

higher value that interposer carries.
4Q26 revenue could be up mid-single digit QoQ with gross  margin at 34-36%.
We stay UW given margin pressure from VSMC into 2027.

Valuation Methodology and Risks
Vanguard International Semiconductor (5347.TWO)
Base case, residual income model. Key assumptions: a cost of equity of 9.2%, a medium-term
growth rate of 10.5%, and a terminal growth rate of 4.0%.
Risks to Upside
n Stronger-than-expected LCD driver IC demand
n Loosened competition in power semi business
n Faster-than-expected EV penetration
n Inability of IDM customers to move production in-house

Risks to Downside
n Inability to fully utilize VSMC 12-inch investment
n Weaker-than-expected LCD driver IC demand
n Intensified competition in power semi business
n Slower-than-expected EV penetration
n IDM customers moving production in-house

STOCK RATINGS
Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy,
Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell.  Investors should carefully read the definitions of all
ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan

Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone.  In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice.  An investor's decision
to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

### Page 6

Inventec (2356.TW)
10 November 2026 Citi Research
6
Inventec
Company description
Established in 1975 and listed in 1996, Taiwan-based Inventec is a key ODM
of NBs, servers, smartphones, and wearable/Io T devices to global customers.
Major shareholders include the founder Mr. Yeh Kuo-I, whose family has (both
directly and indirectly) a c35% stake.
Investment strategy
We rate Inventec shares as Buy. We expect stable demand in NB and server to
help support near-term earnings, while product diversification within smart

devices to areas such as Io T and medical space should help improve L-T
margins. We expect the company's solid cash yield to serve as a downside
cushion.
Valuation
Our target price of NT$65 for Inventec shares is based on 17x 2027 EPS, which
is slightly above the mid-point of its historical trading range, reflecting the
company's product diversification and stable demand outlook led by servers
and commercial NBs, as well as increasing AI server exposure that could

potentially drive margin expansion and better earnings momentum.
Risks
Key downside risks that could impede the stock from reaching our target price
include: 1) Slower-than-expected shipment growth/weaker-than-expected
sell-through in HomePods and AirPods could lead to downside in our sales
forecasts; 2) If competitors adopt aggressive pricing strategies to gain market
shares, our ASP and margin assumptions could face downside risk; 3) Lower-
than-expected server margins driven by rising exposure to Chinese customers

could impact our earnings forecasts negatively; 4) Deterioration in NB
demand and investment in solar would hurt our forecasts; and 5) Stronger-
than-expected Taiwan dollar could lead to FX loss and downside to our
current forecasts.

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.
Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:

Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside
call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds
15% against the direction of the call (unless over-ridden by the analyst). The analyst may also remove a Catalyst Watch or STV
call prior to the end of the specified period in a published research note. A Catalyst Watch/STV Upside or Downside call may be

catalyst(s) and associated share-price movement will not materialize as expected.

股價相對大盤走勢

jpp-KY(5284 TT)  Trading Buy

2027 年營運多點開花。  
投資建議 ：雖 jpp-KY 2026 年面臨客戶規格調整，使得伺服器
機櫃、機構件等產品短期出貨較少的問題，但考量 2027 年航太
部門營運增速有望提升、伺服器部門在多款產品貢獻下，營運
仍能維持一定增速，使得 jpp-KY 2027 年營運多點開花，故給
予 jpp-KY Trading Buy 的投資建議。  
3Q26 雖有客戶規格調整因素、但電源機構件持續出貨，公告財
報表現佳： 3Q26 因客戶規格調整，使得伺服器機櫃、機構件等
產品短期出貨較少，但整體仍受惠電源櫃結構件出貨，營運維
持高檔，使得 jpp-KY 於 11/06/2026 公告的 3Q26 財報表現佳，
3Q26 營收 11.64 億元， QoQ-3.26% ，稅後純益 2.93 億元，
QoQ+25.62% ， EPS 5.31 元。  
2026 年雖有客戶規格調整問題，但電源櫃產品出貨、歐洲子公
司整頓將至，營運維持高檔： 2026 年 jpp 雖面臨客戶規格調整，
使得伺服器機櫃、機構件等產品短期出貨較少，但電源櫃結構
件開始出貨，加上先前併購的第四家法國子公司 Segnere 整頓
預計於 4Q26 進入尾聲，開始提升 jpp 的航太部門營運增速，使
得 jpp 2026 年營運維持高檔，預估 2026 年營收 46.81 億元，
YoY+25.46% ，稅後純益 9.24 億元，YoY+50.42% ，EPS 16.73 元。 
2027 年航太、伺服器等部門業務推動，營運多點開花： 2027 年
隨著歐洲航太營運部門開始成長，加上 2024 年與航太製造商 -
Safran 的合作仍持續進行，研究部參考過往航太產業的產品開
發時程至少需 2-3 年，預期 2027 年起 Safran 等先前開發的產品
將逐步放量，推升 jpp 的航太部門營運增速。而在伺服器部門，
研究部認為規格調整等下游客戶的生產問題終將結束，在客戶
的生產問題結束後，加上 jpp 近年持續於伺服器產品的開發，
預期 jpp 2027 年的伺服器部門營運仍維持一定增速。綜合以上，
考量 jpp 2027 年有望呈現多產品、多部門的營運多點開花，預
估 2027 年營收 56.27 億元， YoY+20.21% ，稅後純益 11.13 億
元， YoY+20.56% ， EPS 20.17 元。

jpp-KY 依循 AA1000SES 利害關係人議合原則、 GRI 準則編列 2025 年永
續報告書，並於 2024 年設置永續發展委員會，透過定期會議的召開，訂定
年度永續發展政策及與責任商業行為相關的承諾政策、並檢視各項永續發
展議。 jpp-KY 透過安裝太陽能光電系統、節能設備，降低企業運營對環境
的影響，而經  ISO 14064-1: 2018 標準盤查的 2025 年溫室氣體排放量為
7,008.73 公噸  CO2e ，較 2024 年增加 12.61% ；溫室氣體排放強度為 1.32 ，
較 2024 年減少 49.43% 。此外， jpp-KY 認為企業的永續發展不僅僅是環保
層面的努力，也包括對員工與社會的承諾，所以 jpp-KY 重視員工的專業成
長與職涯發展，除了提供員工職能與過渡協助的教育訓練方案之外，也透過
定期績效評核制度，提供明確的回饋與發展建議，作為員工能力精進與升遷
發展的參考依據，同時透過建教合作計畫、舉辦全國性自動化比賽，與社會
建立起良性互動。 jpp-KY 相信透過環保、員工福祉與社會責任方面的制度
化建立過程，將所有利害關係人納入企業長期行動、形成企業文化，過程中
不僅提升品牌形象，還能增強員工的忠誠度與使命感，也能為子孫後代創造
一個更可持續的世界。  
公司簡介：  
jpp-KY ( 經寶精密 )成立於 1995 年，專精於航太、通訊 (伺服器、自動販賣
機 )、醫療顯示等利基型板金市場。公司先前為了降低疫情對航太生產衝擊，
除了跨入航太 MRO 的維修市場，並且爭取到空中巴士的客機改貨機訂單，
也積極發展非航太業務，例如 :AI 伺服器、自動販賣機、新能源機車等。 2Q26
產品營收占比，航太 25% 、通訊 65% 、電子 5%、其他 5%。

3Q26 雖有客戶規格調整因素、但電源機構件持續出貨，公告財報表現佳：
3Q26 因客戶規格調整，使得伺服器機櫃、機構件等產品短期出貨較少，但
整體仍受惠電源櫃結構件出貨，營運維持高檔，使得 jpp-KY 於 11/06/2026
公告的 3Q26 財報表現佳， 3Q26 營收 11.64 億元， QoQ-3.26% ，稅後純益
2.93 億元， QoQ+25.62% ， EPS 5.31 元。

### Page 3

3 群益投顧 
4Q26 考量歐美航太淡季將至，營運維持高檔：  
4Q26 雖然電源櫃結構件仍會持續出貨，但研究部考量下游客戶電源產品生
產情形與歐美聖誕假期的航太淡季將至，預期 jpp 4Q26 營運仍維持高檔，
預估 4Q26 營收 11.47 億元， QoQ-1.51% ，稅後純益 1.79 億元， QoQ-39.10% ，
EPS 3.23 元。  
2026 年雖有客戶規格調整問題，但電源櫃產品出貨、歐洲子公司整頓將至，
營運維持高檔：  
2026 年 jpp 雖面臨客戶規格調整，使得伺服器機櫃、機構件等產品短期出
貨較少，但電源櫃結構件開始出貨，加上先前併購的第四家法國子公司
Segnere 整頓預計於 4Q26 進入尾聲，開始提升 jpp 的航太部門營運增速，
使得 jpp 2026 年營運維持高檔 ， 預估2026 年營收 46.81 億元 ，YoY+25.46% ，
稅後純益 9.24 億元， YoY+50.42% ， EPS 16.73 元。  
圖二、 jpp-KY 的 Rack 產品

資料來源： jpp-KY 。  
2027 年航太、伺服器等部門業務推動，營運多點開花：  
2027 年隨著歐洲航太營運部門開始成長，加上 2024 年與航太製造商 -Safran
的合作仍持續進行，研究部參考過往航太產業的產品開發時程至少需 2-3
年，預期 2027 年起 Safran 等先前開發的產品將逐步放量，推升 jpp 的航太
部門營運增速。而在伺服器部門，研究部認為規格調整等下游客戶的生產問
題終將結束，在客戶的生產問題結束後，加上 jpp 近年持續於伺服器產品的
開發，預期 jpp 2027 年的伺服器部門營運仍維持一定增速。綜合以上，考量
jpp 2027 年有望呈現多產品 、 多部門的營運多點開花 ， 預估2027 年營收 56.27
億元， YoY+20.21% ，稅後純益 11.13 億元， YoY+20.56% ， EPS 20.17 元。  
投資建議：  
雖 jpp-KY 2026 年面臨客戶規格調整，使得伺服器機櫃、機構件等產品短期
出貨較少的問題，但考量 2027 年航太部門營運增速有望提升、伺服器部門
在多款產品貢獻下，營運仍能維持一定增速，使得 jpp-KY 2027 年營運多點
開花，故給予 jpp-KY Trading Buy 的投資建議。

7 群益投顧 
  【投資評等說明】  
  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首次評等潛在上漲空間 ≥ 35%  
  買進 (Buy)  15% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <35%  
  區間操作 (Trading Buy) 5% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <15%

中立 (Neutral)  
無法由基本面給予投資評等  
預期近期股價將處於盤整  
建議降低持股

【投資評等調整機制】  
  投資評等係「首次」給予特定個股投資評等時，其前一日收盤價相較 3 個月
目標價之潛在上漲空間計算而得。個股投資評等分為四個等級，定義如上。
爾後的投資評等係依循「首次評等」，直到停止推薦。   
「強力買進」、「買進」及「區間操作」均有 upside 目標價。差別僅在於，
首次評等時潛在報酬率不同。「中立」則無目標價。  
一旦我們給予特定個股「強力買進」 、 「買進」或「區間操作」之投資評等，
就是責任的開始，爾後將透過各式研究報告作定期性、持續性追蹤基本面及
股價變化，直到停止推薦。  
停止推薦情境：  
1.  達目標價。  
2.  雖未達原訂目標價，但檢視基本面、訊息面、籌碼面等多方訊息，研
判股價上漲空間已然有限，將適時出具降評報告。  
3.  推薦後股價不漲反跌。

【免責聲明】  
  本研究報告僅提供予特定人之客戶作為參考資料「非經同意不得轉載」 。我
們並不確保此資訊的完整性與正確性，投資人應了解，報告中有關未來預測
之陳述可能不會實現，因而不應被依賴。而且此報告並非根據特定投資目的
或依預定對象之財務狀況所撰寫出來的，因此，此研究報告的目的，既非對
投資人於買賣證券、選擇權、期貨或其他證券相關之衍生性商品提供詢價服
務，亦非作為進行交易的要約。投資人應注意到相關證券之價值及收益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

## Document 215: [000270] 기아(000270) 배당은 지켜질 것기아(000270) 배당은 지켜질 것배당은 지켜질 것 - 기업

기아 (000270)

Company Brief 
자동차/모빌리티 
Analyst 김준성 
joonsung.kim@meritz.co.kr 
RA 배현서 
hyeonseo.bae@meritz.co.kr 배당은 지켜질 것  
3Q26 지배주주 순이익, 컨센서스 -38% 하회 
매출 31.6조원 (+10% YoY) 으로 컨센서스에 부합했다. 출고 판매 (+10% YoY), 소
매 판매 (+8% YoY) 모두 높은 성장을 기록하며, 외형 확대에 기여했다. 
영업이익 1.82조원 (+24% YoY) 으로 컨센서스를 -25% 하회했다. 전년 동기 대미 
관세 25% 부과에 따른 기저효과 +5,330억원 (2025년 11월부터 15%로 하향)이 
존재했고 기말 환율 (원/달러 -12% QoQ 강세) 변동에 따른 판매보증 충당부채 환
입 +6,700억원을 인식했음에도 불구하고, 신규 출시 저가 BEV 모델들의 판매 확대
에 따른 전사 평균 판매가격 하락  할인 경쟁 심화  원재료 및 운송비 상승으로 부
진한 실적을 기록했다. 
지배주주 순이익 1.29조원 (-10% YoY) 으로 컨센서스를 -38% 하회했다. 영업 실적 
부진과 함께 원화 강세에 의한 외화자산 평가손실 및 관계사 실적 악화에 따른 지분
법 이익 감소 (-36% YoY)가 더해진 결과다.

6,000원 이상 배당 유지 전망 
4Q26 이후 실적 방향도 먹구름이 가득하다. 원/달러 환율의 YoY 강세 반영이 시작
된다. 미국 시장 내 경쟁력 확보를 위해 가장 중요한 원/엔 환율의 방향성 또한 비우
호적 흐름이 지속 중이다. 내수 시장을 포함해 전세계 주요 시장 내 중국산 차량과의 
경쟁 강도도 높아지고 있다. 원유 가격 뿐만 아니라 알루미늄, 구리 등 핵심 원재료 
가격의 상승 반영도 이어지고 있다. 
2026년 연간 지배주주 순이익 6.9조원 (-9% YoY) 을 전망한다. 주주환원 총액은 축
소될 것이다. 다만 자사주 매입을 줄이더라도 배당은 전년 수준을 지킬 것으로 기대
한다. 참고로 2025년 실적에 기반한 주주환원은 자사주 매입 0.9조원, 배당 2.6조원 
(주당 6,800원)이었으며, 주주환원율은 48%였다. 현대차그룹은 Boston Dynamics
에 대한 증자를 이어가고 있다. 정의선 회장도 마찬가지다. 그리고 기아의 배당은 현
대차  현대글로비스의 배당과 함께 증자 재원으로 활용되고 있다. 6,000 원 이상의 
배당 유지를 기대하며, 6% 이상 시가 배당 수익률 제시를 통해 주가 하방 경직성 확
보가 가능하다고 판단한다. 기업가치 상승 재개를 위해서는 기존 차량 제조  판매 사
업을 넘어 피지컬 데이터 디바이스 제공 사업 진출 가시성 확보가 필요하다. 
 Meritz Research  2026. 11. 2

Buy (20거래일 평균종가 대비 상승 여력 기준) 
적정주가 (12개월) 150,000원

## Document 309: Brent at $77_bbl_ 3 calls for energy markets

Source PDF: all report pdf/Brent at $77_bbl_ 3 calls for energy markets.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-11-10
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Given the level of uncertainty in energy markets this year and
with little sign of a change going into next year, any call on
energy markets for 2027 is a low-conviction call. But here are
our 3 calls for energy markets for 2027
Warren Patterson
Oil ﬂows are recovering, but uncertainty remains
How to read our three calls for 2027
Source: ING
ING's base call: Brent to average
$77/bbl next year
Your privacy is important
At ING we respect your right to choose how your data is used.

You can review and change your preferences at any time in
our Cookie policy.

Targeting Cookies
Save preferences Accept all

### Page 2

Oil markets have been on a roller coaster this year given
events in the Middle East. However, we are going into year-
end with oil ﬂows from the region near pre-war levels despite
the lack of a deal between the US and Iran. While oil ﬂows are
normalising, the way oil is coming out of the region is
anything but normal, which is reﬂected in the surge in tanker
shipping rates, given the very ineﬃcient oil ﬂows.
Our base case assumes occasional ﬂare-ups between the US

and Iran, meaning a full normalisation of Persian Gulf
supplies remains some way oﬀ and the oil market is likely to
retain a degree of risk premium. However, provided there are
no signiﬁcant or prolonged disruptions, we expect the market
surplus to build over the course of 2027, leaving Brent to
average around $77/bbl. A deal between the US and Iran
would add further downside risk to this view.
Our risky call: OPEC will see more member
exits
We believe harmony within OPEC could be tested, with the

possibility of further members leaving the group. This follows the
UAE’s departure earlier this year. Post-Persian Gulf disruptions, any
breakdown or weakening of the group risks adding more supply to
the market, which would potentially leave further downside to our
base case price forecasts for Brent in 2027.
The most likely candidate to leave OPEC is Venezuela, with the US’s
growing inﬂuence over the Venezuelan oil industry following the
capture and arrest of President Maduro at the start of 2026. But we

believe that some Persian Gulf producers may also question their
membership, particularly if disagreements emerge over the
capacity numbers used by the group to set production targets. Iraq
has already made noise around production targets, and following
the exit of the UAE, some members may be looking at them
enviously, with the UAE pumping record volumes of crude since
leaving the bloc.

Read more
Download
Our bold call: TTF to trade sub-€40/MWh
before the end of next year
European gas prices have strengthened signiﬁcantly in 2026. But

what goes up must come down, and we believe prices could fall
sharply next year. There are clearly plenty of assumptions here,
most importantly a normalisation of Persian Gulf LNG ﬂows in
early 2027. If that happens, we believe TTF could trade below
€40/MWh by end-2027.
On the demand side, higher prices are already driving some
industrial demand destruction, while we are deep in the gas-to-coal
switching range. Furthermore, El Niño could bring milder weather to

parts of Europe and East Asia. While far from guaranteed, this could
reduce heating demand in winter. So, we potentially come out of
the 2026/27 winter with the EU gas balance not as tight as many
fear, putting downward pressure on prices.
We also have signiﬁcant US LNG export capacity coming onto the
market, which should help with storage ahead of the 2027/28
winter. If the situation in the Persian Gulf normalises, we also have
new capacity starting up in Qatar.

## Document 331: Consumer Panel update Q3 2026_ More resilience (2026-11-12)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Consumer Panel update Q3 2026_ More resilience (2026-11-12).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-12

### Page 1

WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
CONSUMER PANEL Q3 2026 
MORE RESILIENCE
3 November 2026
• Average consumer spend across 
the Westpac-DataX Consumer 
Panel remained resilient, rising 
1.5% in Q3 and 4.6%yr. 
• While some of this reflected a price-
driven surge in fuel spend (+0.3%qtr) 
discretionary categories also posted 
robust gains (contributing 0.9ppts 
to the headline rise) with non-fuel 
essential categories contributing 
positively as well (+0.5ptps).
• Households continued to add to savings 
and buffers. Savings rose 1.3%qtr, led 
by younger households and mortgagors, 
with average savings buffers increasing 
to 22.2 months of mortgage repayments. 
• The detail shows financial pressures 
remain uneven but spending patterns 
appear to be converging across 
consumers with low, medium and high 
buffers. Much of this appears to relate 
to a reduction in fuel consumption.

Westpac–DataX Consumer Panel
The Westpac–DataX Consumer Panel is a large 
dataset that gives a timely and detailed picture 
of Australian consumer finances and behaviour. 
Developed by DataX, Westpac’s data analytics 
team, the dataset links transaction activity with 
balance sheet information to give a complete view 
of income, spending, saving and borrowing flows. 
All data is de-identified and aggregated to 
ensure privacy. The resulting sample of over 
one million customers gives an accurate 
representation of trends across the wider 
Australian consumer and is perfectly suited 
to quickly tracing responses to events like 
interest rate moves and energy price shocks.
Powered by 
Fuel and non-fuel spending lift

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Heatmap of monthly spending categories
The Westpac DataX Consumer Panel  shows average
consumer spending remained resilient in Q3, rising 1.5%
despite a further lift in fuel costs. Fuel contributed 0.3ppts
to quarterly growth, but discretionary spending did more

of the heavy lifting, contributing 0.9ppts as recreation
and travel-related spending recovered. Households also
continued to add to their financial buffers: savings up 1.3%
in the quarter and average buffers across households with
a mortgage increasing to 22.2 months of repayments.
Beneath the aggregate picture, lower-buffer households
remain more exposed to higher fuel costs and mortgage
repayments, but their discretionary spending share is
looking increasingly similar to other households.

Spending holds up despite higher fuel costs
Average household spending increased 1.5% in Q3, with much
of the strength again driven by transport-related spending.
Fuel contributed 0.3ppts to quarterly growth, while transport
excluding fuel contributed a further 0.4ppts. Excluding the
direct effects of the price-driven lift in fuel, average spend
was up 1.2%qtr. Importantly, stronger fuel spending did not
come at the expense of discretionary purchases. Discretionary

categories contributed 0.9ppts to quarterly spending growth,
compared with 0.5ppts from non-fuel essentials.
Solid rise in non-fuel spend and financial buffers
Discretionary spending dominates
Recreation and culture contributed 0.3ppts to growth, reflecting
stronger spending on sporting services, while restaurants and
hotels added a further 0.2ppts. Transport spending was also
boosted by a rebound in airline expenditure, which more than
unwound the weakness recorded in Q2 when refunds relating

to cancellations due to conflict in the Middle East weighed on
the category. Discretionary spending rose 0.9%qtr, following a
flat result and a 0.1% rise in the previous two quarters.
Illiana Jain
Economist, Westpac Group

### Page 3

Past performance is not a reliable indicator of future performance. The forecasts given above are predictive in character. Whilst every effort has been taken to ensure that the assumptions on which the forecasts are based are reasonable,

WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
Savings growth by mortgage status
Households continue to rebuild savings
Savings increased 1.3% in Q3, with the strongest gains again 
recorded among younger households. Savings balances for 
18–34 year olds increased 2.0%qtr, compared with 0.7% 
for those aged 55 and over. Mortgagors also continued to 
accumulate savings more rapidly, recording a 2.8% increase 
compared with 0.7% among non-mortgagors. Savings growth 
among mortgagors has now outpaced non-mortgagors for 
three consecutive years.
Stronger savings have continued to lift mortgage buffers. 
Average buffers (savings divided by the sum of essential 
expenses and minimum mortgage repayments), increased to 
22.2 months of repayments in September. Looking within the 
unit-record sample, median mortgage buffers jumped from 
6.3 months in August to 6.8 months, its highest level since the 
series began in 2019.

Median mortgage buffers
Fuel spending a clear drag on consumers
Fuel spending as a share of total spending
Spending outcomes continued to vary across age groups. 
The squeeze remains most apparent among younger 
households. Fuel spending rose 5.1%qtr for 18–34 year olds, 
against a 1.5%qtr increase in non-fuel spending. By contrast, 
non-fuel spending increased 2.1%qtr among households aged 
55 and over. 
The gap between mortgagors and non-mortgagors was 
more modest. Spending rose 1.6%qtr among mortgagors 
and 1.9%qtr among those without a mortgage, suggesting 
mortgage status alone masks considerable variation in 
household financial positions – a divide that becomes clearer 
when households are grouped by the size of their mortgage 
buffers (see discussion further on in this note). Note that ‘non 
mortgagors’ covers a diverse range of consumers including 
those that are renting and those that own their homes without 
a mortgage.

### Page 4

Past performance is not a reliable indicator of future performance. The forecasts given above are predictive in character. Whilst every effort has been taken to ensure that the assumptions on which the forecasts are based are reasonable, 
the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
4
WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
Median litres of fuel consumed
More importantly, their consumption appears to be less 
responsive to price movements. During periods of cheaper 
fuel, high-buffer households show a noticeably larger lift in 
litres purchased, consistent with stockpiling when prices are 
favourable. Low-buffer households show much less of this 
response, while their fuel volumes have also adjusted less 
during the recent increase in fuel costs.
The spending data show the consequence. Fuel expenditure 
has risen sharply across all three groups recently, but remains 
highest among low-buffer households, where it also absorbs 
a larger share of total spending. Food spending has also 
declined since 2023. This may partly reflect households 
economising within the category, including switching towards 
cheaper or home-brand products, although further work is 
needed to test this.
Taken together, the households with the smallest savings 
cushions face higher mortgage repayments, spend more 
on fuel and appear to have less scope to adjust their fuel 
consumption when prices change. 
Discretionary spend as a share of total
This suggests that in aggregate, household balance sheets 
are looking healthy. The picture is a little at odds with the very 
weak reads on our Westpac-Melbourne Index Consumer 
Sentiment Index which suggests finances are coming under 
much more pressure. The fact that both average spending and 
saving both rose suggests incomes over the quarter were not 
overly stretched and that more of the weakness in sentiment 
may relate to concerns about the outlook (something that may 
also be encouraging consumers to build buffers now, while they 
still can).
Pressures intense for low-buffer households
Aggregate buffer measures mask sizeable differences across 
mortgagors. We can get a sense of these variations by 
combining mortgagors into three broad groups according to 
buffer size, i.e. low, middle and high buffer groups. This shows 
that households with the smallest buffers are also more 
exposed to both interest rates and fuel costs.

The buffer differences are relatively stark, the median savings 
of the low-buffer group just $2k compared to $15k for the 
middle-buffer group and $95k for the high buffer group. 
Low buffer households face median minimum mortgage 
repayments of around $2,600 a month, compared with $2,400 
for the middle buffer group and $2,200 for the highest-buffer 
group. 
Low buffer households also spend more on fuel, at around 
$170 a month compared with $160 for the middle buffer 
group and $150 for the high buffer group.

We can use average pump prices to estimate what this 
expenditure implies for fuel volumes. Low-buffer households 
consume around 70 litres a month, middle-buffer households 
67 litres a month and the high-buffer group around 62 litres. 
This compares to an average of 81, 80 and 70 respectively 
through 2024-25. 
Median fuel spend by customer

### Page 5

Past performance is not a reliable indicator of future performance. The forecasts given above are predictive in character. Whilst every effort has been taken to ensure that the assumptions on which the forecasts are based are reasonable, 
the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
5
WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
Yet despite this greater exposure, the gap in broader spending 
behaviour has narrowed. High-buffer households continue 
to spend more on discretionary categories, averaging around 
$1,940 a month compared with $1,740 for low-buffer 
households. However, their discretionary share has been 
broadly stable since 2023, while the shares of low- and 
middle-buffer households have risen by close to a percentage 
point bringing them more in line with the high-buffer group.

Some of the convergence reflects a longer run down-trend in 
fuel spending that has been more pronounced amongst low 
and middle buffer households. This lowering in fuel spend lifts 
the share available for other categories.

Essential spending excluding fuel, meanwhile, has changed 
little across the three cohorts since 2023. The shift in 
spending patterns has largely occurred on the discretionary 
side.

The result is a somewhat mixed picture. Low-buffer 
households remain considerably more exposed to mortgage 
and fuel costs and have far smaller savings cushions. But their 
discretionary spending has nevertheless moved closer to that 
of better-buffered households, suggesting some recovery in 
spending flexibility despite these constraints. 
Conclusion
Aggregate household finances remain resilient: spending and 
savings both increased in Q3 and mortgage buffers continued 
to rebuild. Discretionary spending has also held up despite the 
sharp increase in fuel costs.

But aggregate resilience masks a much more uneven 
distribution of pressure. Low-buffer households 
simultaneously face higher mortgage repayments, greater fuel 
exposure and much smaller savings cushions. While these 
households have so far maintained discretionary spending, 
their capacity to absorb further pressure is clearly more 
limited.
About the Westpac-DataX Consumer Panel
Developed by DataX, Westpac’s data analytics service for 
institutional and business banking customers, the dataset links 
transaction activity with balance sheet information to give 
a complete view of income, spending, saving and borrowing 
flows. 
The resulting sample of over one million customers provides 
an accurate representation of trends across the wider 
Australian consumer. Monthly observations are available back 
to January 2019 with timely updates provided a few weeks 
after the end of each month. As such, it is perfectly suited to 
quickly tracing the impact and responses to events like interest 
rate moves and energy price shocks.

### Page 3

The obvious outcome from the result on Tuesday is that a
Democrat-controlled Congress will stiﬂe President Trump’s domestic
agenda in his ﬁnal two years in power. A bill has to be approved by
both the House and the Senate before being passed on to the
president to sign or veto. With the Democrats having control of the
legislative agenda in the House and Senate, the president has
already warned that he will likely become “Mr Veto”. The only way
to overcome that would be for the Democrats to secure a two-thirds

supermajority in both houses. That isn't the case after last night's
results.
The consequence is that spending and tax proposals, as with most
domestic legislation, will likely be gridlocked. There are potential
areas of compromise tied to healthcare aﬀordability in exchange
for perhaps more spending on defence, but major tax and spending
changes appear unlikely.
On the one hand, this is often viewed positively, as it provides a
stable backdrop for corporate America. On the other, it means there

is little prospect of meaningful change to the nation’s ﬁscal stance.
While we won’t see any further ﬁscal expansion, large ﬁscal deﬁcits
and deteriorating debt dynamics will continue.
The ﬁscal situation will certainly remain a concern, although hitting
the debt ceiling could act as a temporary brake. The issue is likely
to come to a head by mid-summer 2027, with little scope for
compromise between the two sides in Congress. This risks an
extended government shutdown, potentially exceeding last

October’s shutdown, which depressed GDP growth in 4Q 2025 by a
full percentage point. The Federal Reserve risks “ﬂying blind” again
with no government economic statistics being collated or released.
What the Democrats can seek to achieve
Key sectors of corporate America will be nervous that Democratic
control of important oversight committees could bring greater
scrutiny. More congressional hearings involving AI and technology
business leaders would be a likely outcome, while a strong

performance at the ballot box may embolden local Democratic

Investigations into President Trump, his family and members of his

impeachment proceedings begin in the House, raising the

To be convicted and removed from oﬃce, 67 of the 100 senators
would need to vote in favour, which is unlikely. The Democrats could

further toxifying the political situation in the US. Rather than
pursuing such action against the president himself, Democrats

With Democrats now in control of the Senate, they can block or, at

heads of government agencies, ambassadors and cabinet

members, potentially making it diﬃcult for the president to replace
any senior oﬃcials he chooses to ﬁre.
What will the president do?
A key question is whether a constrained President Trump, entering
the ﬁnal phase of his tenure, becomes more willing to accept
political or market disruption in pursuit of his objectives.
If there is little sense of any compromise, the ensuing gridlock in
Congress would leave the president to focus on areas where he can

exercise executive power: 1) foreign policy and diplomacy, including
trade policy, 2) national security and defence, 3) law enforcement
and regulatory policy, 4) personnel appointments (subject to
Senate approval), and 5) administration and management policy.

The market reaction
The initial bond market reaction has been a fall in yields across the
curve. In the coming days, that could reverse as the prospect of
policy stasis leaves the market weighing a still-elevated ﬁscal
deﬁcit against some easing of inﬂation concerns. We will probably
see front-end yields drift lower, while the back end retains an upside
bias. If we do see more tariﬀs, the back end could ﬁnd a small silver
lining in the additional tax revenues. But any beneﬁt is likely to be

marginal, given the risk that tariﬀs also push inﬂation higher.
Overall, we retain a bias towards the 10-year yield heading towards
5.25%.

since retraced the majority of those losses. Limiting a larger dollar
correction on this news is probably a still-resilient US economy and

at least one more rate hike from the Federal Reserve. The dollar
could see a little more weakness into 2027, however, if an

What it means for Europe and China
With the outcome of the vote limiting President Trump’s options at

undermine economic sentiment in Europe. A new escalation in the
Middle East would push up energy prices and harm the European
economy. Europe’s latest shift towards more protectionist trade

At the same time, with only two years left in oﬃce, the TACO

stronger. If so, some European countries may be tempted to test
the president’s patience and slow the pace of defence spending

administration could try to bring the wars in Iran and Ukraine to an
end. Constrained by Congress and the Supreme Court on trade, it
could ultimately, intentionally or otherwise, end up indirectly
supporting the European economy.

## Document 387: LatAm’s silicon dividend_ tailwinds from the AI investment boom in the U.S. (2026-10-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/LatAm’s silicon dividend_ tailwinds from the AI  investment boom in the U.S. (2026-10-28).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-10-28
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

LatAm’s silicon dividend: 
tailwinds from the AI  
investment boom in the U.S.

1 
Authors 
Fancisco Campos 
Chief LatAm Economist

Beatriz Garcia Nunes 
LatAm Economist

October 28, 2026 
Key messages: 
- U.S. investment has broken away from Europe and China since 2023, 
powered by AI-related equipment, software and research spending. 
- The AI CapEx boom demands commodities and merchandise that LatAm 
can supply.  
- Latin America’s export gains are real but concentrated. Copper, computer 
and electronics products provide the clearest links to the U.S. AI buildout. 
- Chile is the region’s most direct copper beneficiary. Mexico captures the 
windfall stemming from increases hardware demand and trade-diversion. In 
these cases, the AI-related tailwinds have undergirded appreciable 
improvements in external accounts. 
- The cases for nickel and zinc illustrate that the dividend is not widespread. 
U.S. imports of the former have mostly returned toward pre-pandemic levels, 
while for the latter bilateral gains partly reflect supplier substitution. 
- While expanding AI use, supply shortages and incentives can extend the 
CapEx boom, its financial viability poses downside risks. 
Another ‘American exceptionalism’ story  
Investment has become one of the sharpest dividing lines among the world’s 
core economic blocs. Since 2024, U.S. gross fixed capital formation has firmed 
up appreciably. Meanwhile, investment in Europe has broadly stalled while in 
China’s it has retreated, the drivers of which are beyond the scope of this report 
(Figure 1).  
The U.S. divergence has largely been an AI story: a burst of spending on 
computing power, digital infrastructure and intellectual property has offset 
weakness in conventional construction and housing. Indeed, total U.S. fixed 
investment is nearly 12% above its 2023 level. Within it, investment in 
information-processing equipment has risen by roughly 57%, software outlays by 
about 35%, and research and development by around 15%. By contrast, non-
residential structures and residential investment sit below their 2023 levels 
(Figure 2).

### Page 2

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 2

Figure 1: The global investment divergence

Figure 2: AI-related categories lead U.S. investment

Source: US Bureau of Economic Analysis, Eurostat, National Bureau of Statistics of 
China, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Source: US Bureau of Economic Analysis, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Latin America’s commodity channel 
AI is not merely a semiconductor story. Chips require specialized minerals; data 
centers require electricity and cooling; electricity requires grids, transformers and 
storage. That chain pulls in copper, aluminum, silver, tin, lithium and selected 
semiconductor inputs, along with natural gas and uranium. Latin America has 
meaningful exposure to several of these inputs, but copper has emerged as the 
commodity with the cleanest and most economically significant regional link. 
Copper is used throughout the AI infrastructure stack, including power cables, 
substations, transformers, server boards, heat sinks and cooling systems. Rising 
U.S. copper demand has lifted imports from Chile and Peru, particularly the 
former (Figure 3). T he effect matters beyond merchandise export volumes. 
Favorable terms-of-trade have helped Chile generate a current -account surplus 
for the first time in roughly fifteen years (0.2% of GDP as of Q2 2026 on a 4 -
quarter rolling basis ) and have allowed Peru to log the largest surplus in about 
forty-five years (4.5% of GDP as of Q2 2026 on a 4-quarter rolling basis).

28 October 2026

### Page 3

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 3

Figure 3: Copper epitomized the commodity link

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Not every metal is copper 
The broader mining story demands more caution, though. U.S. nickel imports have 
recovered toward pre-Covid levels, but the upswing has not displayed the same 
persistence as copper. Mexico has captured a disproportionate share of the 
bilateral improvement, yet that gain looks more like normalization than a 
sustained AI -driven break (Figure 4). Zinc tells a similar story. U.S. real imports 
stand shy of their pre-pandemic levels, and stronger purchases from Mexico and 
Peru partly reflect a reallocation away from Brazil rather than a decisive increase 
in U.S. demand (Figure 5).

Figure 4: Nickel: recovery, not a boom

Figure 5: Zinc: a substitution story

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

28 October 2026

### Page 4

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 4

Mexico’s hardware advantage 
The larger non-commodity prize is in computers and electronics. U.S. imports of 
these products have sky -rocketed, while the supplier mix has shifted away from 
China. Mexico and various Asian economies have gained share on the back of this 
double-whammy impulse (Figure 6). 
This helps explain why Mexico’s non-automotive manufacturing exports, notably 
electronics, have outpaced automotive shipments to the U.S. and pressures from 
higher oil prices. The resulting non -oil trade surplus has offset pressure from the 
oil balance and helped Mexico generate a balanced current account (0% of GDP 
as of Q2 2026 on a 4 -quarter rolling basis). The external boost is meaningful, 
although it sho uld not necessarily be interpreted as bona fide  evidence of 
nearshoring, in our view.

Figure 6: U.S. computer & electronics imports going into overdrive

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research.

How much runway remains? 
Quantifying or mapping out a timeframe for the AI investment outlook is beyond 
the scope of this piece or our expertise. Nevertheless, we’d venture to argue that 
the capex cycle appears more likely to mature gradually than to stop abruptly . 
One reason is that the investment case is shifting from frontier -model training 
toward enterprise inference . Capacity that once served development can 
increasingly run commercial workloads for companies. That creates a path to 
revenue even if artificial general intelligence (AGI) crystallizes later than 
expected. It also spreads demand to a much larger corporate customer base.  
A second reason is scarcity. Graphics processing units (GPUs) remain tight, while 
power availability, grid connections, cooling equipment, construction capacity 
and data -center complexity limit how quickly supply can respond. Bottlenecks 
may therefore exte nd the cycle. The constraint is especially relevant for copper 
28 October 2026

### Page 5

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 5

and power infrastructure, where projects have long lead times and permitting 
hurdles. 
A third reason is strategic. AI development resembles a prisoner’s dilemma at 
both the corporate and national levels. Each participant may worry about costs, 
safety and control, yet slowing unilaterally risks surrendering technological, 
commercial or natio nal security advantages. The same tension is visible in calls 
for guardrails alongside continued spending. Competition can therefore sustain 
capex even when short-term returns are uncertain. 
We acknowledge the risks, nonetheless. Higher borrowing costs raise the hurdle 
rate for an infrastructure buildout financed partly through debt. More important, 
revenues must eventually justify the installed capital base. The bearish case is not 
that AI la cks value; it is that infrastructure spending may run ahead of 
monetization, compromising some companies’ repayment capacity. Public 
opposition to the development of AI infrastructure and/or adoption of AI 
technologies could also slow down the technology’s roll-out. 
The regional balance  
Summing up, the AI investment boom in the U .S. is delivering a dividend to parts 
of Latin America, not the region as a whole. Chile and Peru benefit through copper 
prices and export volumes. Mexico benefits through electronics manufacturing 
and the replacement of Chinese supply. For Brazil and Colombia, the AI dividend 
is rather muted. Where materialized, furthermore, the dividend supports trade 
and current accounts more clearly rather than improving the domestic 
investment. 
These dynamics should persist while U.S. AI investment remains supply -
constrained and strategically protected. If widespread adoption advances and 
the strategic race intensifies, the impulse can last. If financing costs rise further 
or monetization disappo ints, the cycle may narrow. Either way, copper and 
Mexican electronics remain the region’s most visible gauges of the U.S. AI 
investment appetite.

28 October 2026

### Page 6

Deutsche Bank Research Institute 
LatAm’s silicon dividend: tailwinds from the AI investment boom in the U.S. – October 28, 2026

• As polls suggested, the election was a nailbiter

paper in a significantly enlarged parliament.
• The centre-right bloc of National, NZ First
and ACT won 67 seats. The centre-left bloc
of Labour, Greens and Te Pāti Māori won 57
seats. There was a swing away from National
to its coalition partners while the centre left
grouping picked up a few seats.

• The Opportunity Party delivered on its polling
and will enter parliament with at least 7 seats,
reducing what could have been a larger swing
to the centre-left grouping.
• The size of parliament will expand by around 9
seats as National held onto a large share of its
electorate votes, entitling it to more seats than
implied by its party vote share. As a result,
a bloc would require 66 seats to command a
majority in parliament.
• Several senior National ministers have not

been re-elected, including Finance Minister
Nicola Willis. She will continue in a caretaker
role but will need to be replaced in any new
centre-right government.
• Significant uncertainty about the result exists.
National might lose perhaps two marginal
electorates in the final analysis. There are
400,000 special votes to count which may
shift the list seat allocations as these tend
to reduce the share of the centre-right bloc
by 1.6-1.7ppts. Hence, when all is said and

done, the centre-right’s 10 seat advantage on
election night could reduce by 1-4 seats.
• Should this play out the centre-right would not
have sufficient seats to reach a majority in an
enlarged house and would require the support
of the Opportunity Party to govern.
• The final election outcome will not be
known until 27 November. Serious coalition
negotiations likely won’t begin until then
and delays could occur. Should recounts be
required, some further delay is possible.

• Based on these results, we see a higher
probability of a centre-right government
ultimately emerging that likely will require
the support of the Opportunity Party in some
form. The Opportunity Party likely won’t enter
Cabinet but may offer support on confidence
and supply in exchange for policy concessions.
• But a centre-left government can’t be ruled
out if special votes heavily favour the centre
left bloc, and especially if an agreement
between the Opportunity Party and the centre-

right parties can’t be reached.
• Regardless of the final count, the results
suggest a government with weak support and
ongoing vulnerabilities. Hence the prospect of
controversial large changes may be modest.
For a centre-right government that might
limit the extent of fiscal consolidation, while
the centre left’s plans for larger scale tax and
spending initiatives could be trimmed back.
• A centre-right government won’t change the
RBNZ mandate while a centre-left government

likely still will. A new finance minister will be
required with the possibility of ACT leader
David Seymour taking on that role.
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
New Zealand 2026 General Election:
Preliminary results and next steps.
9 Nov 2026 |  Westpac Economics Team | westpac.co.nz/economics | economics@westpac.co.nz
Kelly Eckhold, Chief Economist | +64 9 348 9382 | +64 21 786 758 | kelly.eckhold@westpac.co.nz

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
The result.
The preliminary results of the 2026 General Election,
based on the election night count, are set out in the table
above. In summary:
• The centre-right bloc of National, NZ First and ACT
won 67 seats.
• The centre-left bloc of Labour, Greens and Te Pāti
Māori won 57 seats.
• The Opportunity Party delivered on its polling and will
enter parliament with 7 seats.
These results indicate a swing away from National to its

coalition partners while the centre-left grouping picked
up a few seats. The big gainer was the Opportunity Party
who perhaps took a big share of what otherwise could
have been a larger swing to the centre-left grouping.
The size of parliament will expand by around nine seats,
as National held onto a large share of its electorate
votes and hence has more seats than it would have been
entitled to based on its party vote share. As a result, a
governing bloc would require 66 seats to command a

majority in parliament.
Several of the electorate seats are very tight (see Table
2), so election night majorities could easily be overturned
once special votes are counted. Of particular relevance
is the seat of Wairarapa, where the National candidate is
leading the Labour candidate by just 139 votes. If Labour
were to win that seat (specials favoured Labour in 2020
but National in 2023), all else equal the centre-right seat
count would decline to 66 seats in what would become

a 130-seat Parliament – just enough to command a
majority. But if either NZ First or ACT were to lose a single
party vote seat to the left bloc once specials are counted
then that majority would disappear.
On that score, historically the election night results have
tended to overstate the support of the centre-right bloc
and understate the support for parties on the left of
the political spectrum. For example, over the previous
three elections (2017, 2020, 2023), the current centre-

right bloc’s vote share has declined 1.9%, 1.7% and 1.6%
respectively following the counting of special votes, with
the National Party losing 2 seats on each occasion once
the official results were declared (Labour, the Greens and
Te Pāti Māori have benefited). On this occasion, given its
overhang, a lower party vote share would not lower the
National Party’s final vote count, although it could alter
the size of its overhang and thus the number of seats

required to command a majority. In recent elections both
NZ First and ACT have seen their vote shares decline
following the counting of special votes.
The process from here.
Official results.
The official results will be declared on 27 November,
adding the results of an estimated 400,000 special votes
(with around a quarter of these cast overseas) to the
2.41m votes counted on election night.
We expect discussions between potential coalition
partners will begin immediately to start sketching out the

parameters of a coalition agreement. But given the tight
margins of the current vote count, no firm positions will
be agreed until the results are declared.
The writ and recounts.
After 27 November, the writ is scheduled to be returned
on 3 December – formally certifying who has been
elected. However, that date is subject to any recounts

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
that might be requested. Parliament is required to meet
no later than 14 January (six weeks after the return of the
writ), providing an opportunity for a new government
to test that it has the confidence of the House. It is
worth noting that Parliament can be summoned and
begin functioning even if coalition negotiations are still
occurring. If no coalition or confidence-and-supply
arrangement has been reached by then, negotiations can

simply continue and the incumbent Government remains
in office under the caretaker convention (Ministers in
the outgoing government retain executive authority
temporarily, even if they are no longer MPs).
Given that the Pre-Election Economic and Fiscal
Update was released in late September there is no legal
requirement for the Treasury to publish a Half Year
Economic and Fiscal Update (HYEFU) this year. And given
that it will be some weeks before a government is formed,

it seems unlikely that there will be a government in place
that would request that a HYEFU be produced despite
there being no statutory requirement (certainly there
would be little time to incorporate policy changes that
fall out of new coalition agreements, thus diminishing
its value). Once a government is formed, it will have an
opportunity to lay out its immediate priorities in the
Budget Policy Statement (BPS), which by law needs to
be released before the end of March next year. The new

government could decide to release an interim fiscal
update with the BPS if that was thought necessary.
Potential coalition outcomes.
We see three main likely outcomes:
• A three-party majority coalition between National, ACT
and NZ First.
• A three-party majority coalition between National, ACT
and NZ First with the support of the Opportunity Party
on confidence and supply.
• Some coalition of Labour, the Greens, Opportunity and
Te Pāti Māori, potentially with Opportunity and Te Pāti

Māori sitting outside the cabinet.
Our preview of the election discussed the policies of
the main political parties, while the figure on page 5
highlights some points of commonality with the centre-
right and centre-left blocs.
National, ACT and NZ First share a broadly pro-
development and deregulatory agenda, including support
for greater resource development, fewer regulatory
barriers and compulsory or expanded KiwiSaver. Beyond
that common core, National and ACT align particularly

closely on lower taxes and deregulation, while National
and NZ First share an emphasis on infrastructure, regional
development and law and order. ACT and NZ First favour
tighter immigration settings and tighter welfare policies.
The differences are clearest at the edges: ACT advocates
deeper tax cuts, smaller government and more market-
led provision, while NZ First places greater emphasis
on economic sovereignty, regional development and
targeted support for working New Zealanders. National

occupies more of the middle ground, combining fiscal
discipline and a pro-business agenda with increased
infrastructure and housing supply.
The Opportunity Party presents itself as a middle
alternative that can work with the left or the right
groupings. They have five bottom line policies they say
they need to support any new government. These include
competition reform through enhanced Commerce
Commission powers, more restrictions on political
lobbyists and donors, a ten-year plan of investment in the

healthcare sector, an end to bottom trawling in inshore
areas and a phase out elsewhere and for the Government
to recommit to New Zealand’s Paris Agreement
obligations. Their success in meeting these bottom
lines with a new government and their flexibility around
these requirements will be key to their ability to form an
agreement with either side. There is better congruence
of the Opportunity Party’s policies with the left than the
right – so progress here will be critical to watch should

their support be required to form a new government. We
suspect they will be very motivated to avoid the need for
a new election via a hung parliament should push come
to shove.

Westpac New Zealand Economics
Labour, the Greens and Opportunity share support
for increased public and social investment, a broader
tax base, greater infrastructure investment, stronger
competition policy, cheaper public transport and a
more active role for government. Labour and the Greens
overlap particularly on higher taxation, public investment
and stronger climate and environmental policy, while
Labour and Opportunity share an emphasis on social

investment and higher public-service wages. The Greens
and Opportunity both favour broader taxation and
stronger climate action, although their preferred tax
instruments differ. Their distinctive priorities are also
clear: Labour places more weight on worker protections
and restoring the RBNZ’s dual mandate; the Greens on
environmental protection, progressive taxation and Te
Tiriti; and Opportunity on land-value taxation, housing
infrastructure, productivity, innovation and evidence-

based policy.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
3
OVERVIEW
Coming into the home strait for 2026,
the Australian consumer has again
got the staggers. Spending has held
up surprisingly well given the intense
headwinds that have buffeted household
budgets over the year. But the same cannot
be said for sentiment which has fallen off
badly in the face of more shocks.

Our October Red Book surveys the scene.
Psychologically, consumers has seen a
return to the ‘cost of living nightmare’
they struggled with through much of
2022, 2023 and 2024. Global forces
and domestic factors are again having
pervasive negative impacts on budgets.
The scale may not be as severe as the
previous episode – inflation is running in
the 3-4% range not the 7-8% and interest
rates have risen 1ppt not 4 – but the
feeling is similar. In part this because there

has was very little reprieve in between –
despite tax cuts and a partial unwind of the
interest rate tightening in 2025, household
incomes never got a chance to regain the
lost ground.
The inflation ‘relapse’, and the difficulty
the RBA and governments seem to be
having in managing the situation, is likely
feeding into fears about the outlook for the
wider economy as well.
Exactly what this means for spending is
not entirely clear. As discussed in our last

report , sentiment reads have been much
weaker than actual spending in recent
years. The apparent disconnect is likely
due to a mix of legacy effects from COVID,
and the specific challenges posed by high
inflation (including the lingering effects
of high prices even once inflation has
subsided).
On balance we expect the spending side
to slow materially, particularly as we
head into year end. Indeed, the seven
weeks between now and Christmas
could prove decisive for the consumer

outlook, beginning with another important
sentiment survey update and then the high
season for retail. How spending performs
outside of key sales windows will be most
telling.
Staggering into year end

1. Consumer spending: slowdown ahead
“... sentiment ... has fallen off badly in the face of

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
4
The Westpac–Melbourne Institute
Consumer Sentiment Index declined 4.2%
over the three months to Oct, unwinding a
mini-rally through the middle of the year.
At 80.4 the average read over the Oct
survey implies that pessimists outnumber
optimists by nearly 20%, a deeply
pessimistic level by historical standards

that is amongst the forty worst reads since
the survey began in the 1973.
The survey detail shows concerns are still
primarily about the cost-of-living and high
interest rates than the more acute job and
insolvency problems that arise during a
recession. The RBA’s Sep rate hike, mid-
way through the survey week looks to have
badly rattled consumers.
The component detail shows a broad
pull-back but with bigger declines in
assessments of family finances and

buyer sentiment and milder declines in
assessments of the economic outlook.
Consumers remain intensely risk averse.
The Westpac Risk Aversion Index held near
historical highs in Sep, readings still only a
touch of the 52-year high of 60.8 recorded
in June 2023. Recall that the index is based
on responses to questions on the ‘wisest
place for savings’. Responses show ‘safe
options’ remain very heavily favoured.
The sentiment mix points to a significant

weakening in spending momentum is
weakening. CSI±, a modified indicator
that shows a better correlation with per
capita spending, points to a shift back to
significant outright contractions in per
capita spending. That said, spending
in 2026 has held up better than these
indicators have suggested so far.
Consumers are on high alert for interest
rate. The Westpac–Melbourne Institute
Mortgage Rate Expectations Index rose
5.5% to 179 over the three months to

Oct, just shy of May’s cycle peak of 181.
The RBA has raised interest rates 25bps
since then. The Nov survey update due Nov
10 will be an important update on how
expectations have evolved.
The ‘time to buy a major item’ index
dropped 4.3% over the last three months,
taking it back near the lows seen earlier
in the year and a full 40pts below the
long run avg. Durables spend has been
mixed over the first half of 2026, posting a
surprisingly solid gain in Q2 but with most

of this centred on new vehicle spend where
‘extenuating circumstances’ were at play.
Homebuyer sentiment has been
understandably unsettled in recent months
given heightened uncertainty around
interest rate and tax policy changes amid
evidence of a broadening downturn in
established markets.
The ‘time to buy a dwelling’ index actually
lifted 3.6% over the three months to Oct
but is still at firmly pessimistic levels. Both
monthly reads and sub-group detail have

been very unsettled.
The broadening and deepening downturn
in Australia’s housing markets is seeing
an equally rapid shift in consumer house
price expectations although these have not
turned as negative as they did during the
2022-23 correction.
The Westpac–Melbourne Institute
Consumer House Price Expectations Index
declined a further 2.4% over the 3mths to
Oct, following a steep 25% drop over the
previous 3mths. At 115.6, the Oct index
read is miles below the 15yr high of 174 at

the start of the year and below the long run
avg of 130. That said, the read is still above
100, meaning more consumers expect
prices to rise than fall.
The labour market remains an island of
relative calm for consumers. The Westpac–
Melbourne Institute Unemployment
Expectations Index rose 9.3% over the
three months to Oct, reversing most of
the 12.1% improvement over the previous
three months (recall that higher reads mean
more consumers expect unemployment to

rise over the year ahead). At 142.2, the Oct
index read is now decisively above the long
run avg of 129 again but  still well below
the levels that would sound more worrying
alarms.
As noted, the Nov survey, currently in the
field and due to be released on Nov 10,
will provide critical updates on many of
these themes.
Quick run-down

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
5
THE CONSUMER MOOD
• Consumers remain stuck in a cost-of-
living nightmare that seems to have
not end in sight. Sentiment remains
deeply pessimistic, weighed down by
continued uncertainty about the path
for inflation and interest rates, the
latter providing a clear negative ‘jolt’
midway through the Oct survey week.

• The Westpac Melbourne Institute
Index of Consumer Sentiment declined
4.2% over the three months to Oct,
unwinding a mini-rally through the
middle of the year. At 80.4 the average
read over the Oct survey implies that
pessimists outnumber optimists by
nearly 20%, a deeply pessimistic level
by historical standards. It is amongst
the forty worst reads since the survey
began in the 1973. We have had
two others in this unfortunate group
already this year (in Apr and Jun) with

another eight in 2022–23. Altogether
this marks the worst period of recurring
extremely weak sentiment since the
disastrous recession in the early 1990s.
• The survey detail shows consumer
concerns are still primarily about the
cost-of-living and high interest rates
than the more acute job and insolvency
problems that arise during a recession.
• Average weekly pump prices pushed
back over $2.30/litre nationally in early
Oct, rising to over $2.35/litre since. This

is near the peaks seen back in April and
up nearly 25% since the start of the
year. Meanwhile the RBA interest rate
rise in late Sep took the cash rate to
4.6%, the highest since 2011, with the
standard variable mortgage interest
rate pushing above 9% for the first time
since 2008.
• The RBA’s Sep move badly rattled
consumers. Sentiment amongst the
60% of the sample surveyed prior came
in at 86.9, up slightly from the Sep
read. The sentiment read across the

40% surveyed after the RBA decision
came in at just 67.2, an alarmingly weak
read that, for complete surveys, has
only been registered during the depths
of the early 1990s recession. The nearly
20% drop between pre and post RBA
samples is also the largest negative
swing we have seen since we started
tracking daily responses in 2019.
• The RBA has delivered another hike as
we go to press, meaning Nov is likely
to see yet another interest rate hike

to sentiment (the survey result will be
released on Nov 10).
Cost-of-living nightmare continues

3. Consumer sentiment: finances, economic conditions

### Page 6

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
6
THE CONSUMER MOOD
• The component detail shows a broad
pull-back but with bigger declines in
assessments of family finances and
buyer sentiment. The ‘finances vs a
year ago’ sub-index dropped 6% over
the 3mths to Oct  to just 66.9, on a par
with April’s extreme low. The pressure
is not expected to ease up any time

soon, the ‘finances, next 12 months’
sub-index also down 8.3% to 88.4, in
the bottom 10% of reads for this sub-
index since the survey began.
• Consumer views on the economy
recorded milder declines, the ‘economy,
next 12 months’ sub-index unchanged
on Jul, holding at 78.3 and the
‘economy, next 5 years’ sub-index down
1.8% to 85.5. However, the latter is now
back near the lows seen in Aug 2020.
This was a dark time during the COVID
crisis when Melbourne had entered

its second lockdown and just prior to
the Federal government significantly
expanding its JobKeeper supports. The
readings suggest there are shades of
COVID now with a similar sense of
helplessness – that Australia is unable
to avoid a global inflation problem and
that there is relatively little authorities
can do to help the situation that does
not add to pressures on consumers.
• The cost of living and interest rate
pressures are both broad and intense.

Pessimists outnumber optimists in 102
of the 106 sub-groups we track, and by
over 20% in nearly two thirds of these.
This speaks to the pervasive influence
of higher fuel costs in particular.
• That said, sentiment is becoming more
dispersed. It is possible to compute
sentiment index scores for each
individual respondent. Chart 4 shows
how the spread across these scores
has evolved since 1995. The average
standard deviation over the full history
is 46pts. It was about 4pts lower over

the decade prior to COVID but has risen
steadily since to over 52pts in Oct, the
average spread over the last 12mths
marking a record high. Chart 5 shows
the full distribution of individual scores
in the last 3mths compared to history.
• We noted in our last report that the
widening spread has been accompanied
by a notable divergence in sentiment
across major and minor party voters.
There are likely multiple underlying
reasons for the shift which the voter

group split may be picking up indirectly
rather than directly due to the way these
skew across different demographics.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
7
Spending: squeeze ahead
• Our CSI± composite combines sub-
indexes tracking views on ‘family
finances’ and ‘time to buy a major
item’ with the Westpac Consumer Risk
Aversion Index and usually provides a
good guide to trends in spending over
the next 3–6mths.
• However, as discussed in detail in our
last report the composite has been of

more limited use in recent years due
to both the volatile spending around
COVID lock-downs and the nature of
the post-COVID cost-of-living shock
to sentiment. Analysis shows adding
the WestpacMelbourne Institute Index
of Unemployment Expectations (p15)
to the composite goes some way to
redressing these issues. Accordingly,
our CSI± indicator will now include this
additional component going forward.
As Chart 6 highlights, the composite
should still mainly be viewed as a

directional guide to where per capita
spending growth momentum is heading
rather than a point estimate.
•  Unsurprisingly, the CSI± indicator fell
steeply over the 3mths to Oct, dropping
from 75.6 to 67.6, putting the measure
over 26pts below its long run avg of
93.8.
• The move reflected the more marked
falls in assessments of finances
and ‘time to buy’ with a significant
deterioration in unemployment
expectations as well. Risk aversion
remained unchanged at very high levels.

• Chart 6 shows the current read is
consistent with a large contraction in
real per capita consumer spending,
even allowing for the wedge that has
opened up between observed spending
growth and the CSI± indicator over the
last few years.
• The Q2 national accounts showed some
slowing in consumer spending although
it was more resilient than feared to the
combined effect of higher fuel prices
and interest rates while incomes posted
a robust rise. More generally a range

of shocks and shifts made for a messy
quarter (see here for more).
• Real consumption rose 0.4%qtr taking
annual growth to 1.8%yr, down from
a peak of 2.7%yr in Q3 last year. The
detail showed declines in fuel spend
and transport services (including air
travel) were more than offset by a 10%
surge in vehicle sales, led by a big rise
in EVs and hybrids.

SENTIMENT INDICATORS
